**Theorem.** Over any characteristic-zero field $K$, the field generated by $E,W,R$ contains no regular reflection-invariant functions of the sphere coordinates except those independent of the sphere. Precisely,
$$
           K(E,W,R)\cap K[p,q,t,t^{-1}]=K[t,t^{-1}].
$$
This also holds for regularity on the full octahedral product. A function in $K(p,q,t)$ regular on that product descends through the left circle quotient and then through inversion, since it is invariant under both. The regular ring of the resulting reflection quotient is $K[p,q,t,t^{-1}]$.

In the function field, $E,W$ recover $t,v$ by
$$
                    T=\frac{E}{E-(1-W)^N},\qquad v=\frac{W}{T^2}.
$$
The joint field is therefore $K(t,v,R)$. Introduce $u=p(2A(v)-p)$. Since $R=(A(v)^2-u)/B(v)^2$, this is also the field $K(t,v,u)$. It is the fixed field of the reflection
$$
                    p\longmapsto 2A(v)-p
$$
with $v,t$ fixed. Every regular polynomial in $p,q,t,t^{-1}$ is in $K[t,t^{-1},v,p]$ after substituting $q=vp/9$. The fixed ring of the reflection on this polynomial ring is $K[t,t^{-1},v,u]$. This follows by shifting $p$ by $A(v)$ and taking the even polynomials.

It remains to impose regularity at $p=0$. With $q,t$ generic, $v=9q/p$ has pole order one and
$$
                    \frac{u}{v}
     =\frac{p(2A(v)-p)}{v}\ \longrightarrow\ -\frac34q .
$$
If a polynomial in $v,u$ has positive total degree $d$ with homogeneous leading part $H_d$, its coefficient of $v^d$ at this divisor is $H_d(1,-3q/4)$. This is nonzero because $q$ is transcendental over $K(t)$. Thus the polynomial has a pole. The only regular elements of the joint field are the Laurent scalars, as asserted. Their pure phases are safe by the Laurent theorem, since a single strict sign in the Laurent support also gives eventual marked vanishing with coefficients in the full compact coordinate ring.

The signed conditional coordinate $(p-A(v))/\sqrt{B(v)^2}$ does not change this obstruction. Its joint law with $v$ is the independent arcsine law on $[-1,1]$ and the squared-uniform law on $[0,1]$. But $B(v)^2$ is nonsquare in $K(t,v,p)$, since it has odd order at $v=1$. Conjugating the square root reverses the signed coordinate and fixes $E,W$. Any rational expression in the signed coordinate and $E,W$ that descends to the original function field is therefore even in the signed coordinate. It belongs to $K(E,W,R)$ and the same intersection applies.

The obstruction also survives adjoining both normalized circle coordinates on the rotation quotient. To make that statement precise, put $a_\ell=x_\ell^2$ and use the rotation invariant
$$
    J=x_1x_2x_3(a_1-a_2)(a_1-a_3)(a_2-a_3),\qquad
    d=p-A(v),\qquad s=\frac{3J}{2p}.
$$
The ring of the rotation quotient is $K[p,q,J]$ with $J^2$ equal to $q$ times the discriminant of $Z^3-Z^2+pZ-q$. Indeed, a rotation-invariant polynomial splits under any reflection into a reflection-invariant part and a part transforming by the determinant. The latter vanishes on every reflecting hyperplane, so the product of their linear equations, namely $J$ up to scalar, divides it. The quotient by that product is reflection-invariant. Restriction to the unit sphere preserves this description by invariant projection. Substitution of $q=vp/9$ gives
$$
                 s^2=v(B(v)^2-d^2).
$$
Inversion preserves $v,p$ and reverses $J$. Together with the conditional arcsine law this identifies $(d,s)$ at fixed $v$ with
$$
                     \big(B(v)\cos\theta,\ \sqrt v B(v)\sin\theta\big)
$$
for uniform $\theta$. For instance, the unnormalized mixed moments are
$$
 \mathbb E[d^{2i}s^{2j}\mid v]
     =v^j B(v)^{2i+2j}\frac{(1/2)_i(1/2)_j}{(1)_{i+j}},
$$
and they vanish whenever either exponent is odd. This gives the joint functional on the full rotation-invariant angular ring. Applying the endpoint proof to any polynomial in the two normalized angular coordinates instead of $R^j$ gives the same pure and marked identities with the circle average of that polynomial.

Nevertheless, every rational expression in
$$
                 E,\quad W,\quad \sqrt v,\quad d/B(v),
                             \quad s/(\sqrt v B(v))
$$
that descends to the original rotation quotient and is regular on the product is a Laurent scalar. Over $K(t,v,\sqrt v)$ the conditional conic $d^2+(s/\sqrt v)^2=B(v)^2$ is geometrically integral. Its function field therefore does not make the nonsquare $B(v)^2$ into a square. The nonsquareness over $K(t,v,\sqrt v)$ follows from odd order at $v=1$. The automorphism reversing $B(v)$ and fixing the original function field acts on the displayed generators in the same way as the conditional half-turn $(d,s)\mapsto(-d,-s)$ fixing both square roots. A descended function in these generators is thus fixed by that half-turn.

In the regular rotation-invariant Laurent ring write such a function as $H_0(p,q,t)+JH_1(p,q,t)$, with polynomial dependence on $p,q$ and Laurent dependence on $t$. The even and odd parts under inversion can be considered separately, since inversion commutes with the half-turn. The even part is scalar by the preceding reflection test. For the odd part, the half-turn reverses $s=3J/(2p)$. Hence $pH_1(p,vp/9,t)$ is an odd polynomial in $d=p-A(v)$ with coefficients in $K[t,t^{-1},v]$. It is therefore $(p-A(v))H(v,u,t)$ for a polynomial in $v,u$ with Laurent coefficients in $t$. As $p\to0$ at generic $q,t$, the left side vanishes, while $p-A(v)$ has a pole of order two. If $H$ is nonzero, its highest part in $v,u$ gives a nonzero leading coefficient at that pole by $u/v\to-3q/4$. This is impossible and the odd part is zero.

### The two octahedral arrangements and a uniform conditional coordinate

The three coordinate mirrors and the six long-root mirrors of $B_3$ give different rational heights. On the unit sphere, put $p=p_2$, $q=p_3$, and let $f$ be the discriminant of $Z^3-Z^2+pZ-q$. Set
$$
 r=1-3p,\qquad k=p-9q,\qquad X=\frac{9q}{p},\qquad V=\frac{9f}{rk}.
$$
Here $X$ is the height from the coordinate mirrors, and $V$ is the squared height from the long-root mirrors. In fact the product of the long-root linear forms is, up to sign, the Vandermonde in the three squared coordinates. Its square is $f$, and the squared norm of its gradient is $4rk$ on the sphere, by expansion in the elementary symmetric functions.

**Theorem.** The joint law of $(X,V,1-X-V)$ is Dirichlet with parameters $(1/2,1/2,1/2)$. There is also a joint law retaining all the reflection-invariant coordinates. Put
$$
       S=\frac{2rp}{k},\qquad
       B=\frac{X}{5-2(S+S^{-1})}.
$$
Then $\sqrt S$ is uniform on $[1/\sqrt 2,\sqrt 2]$ and $B$ is independent of $\sqrt S$ with the arcsine law on $[0,1]$. The original angular coordinates are recovered by
$$
       p=\frac{(2-S)(1+(2S-1)B)}{6},\qquad
       q=\frac{(2-S)(2S-1)}{9S}\,Bp.                              \tag{H14}
$$
Thus every joint moment of $p,q$ is an integral against an independent uniform and arcsine pair. No identification of two sheets is required in (H14).

To prove this, use the density $(qf)^{-1/2}$ in $(p,q)$, up to normalization. In $(r,k)$ the equation of the level $V=v$ is
$$
        27f=-9k^2+12rk+4r^3-4r^2=3vrk.
$$
Put $u=6k/r-3+v$ and $L^2=(1-v)(9-v)$. The inverse formulas are
$$
 \begin{aligned}
 r&=\frac{(u-1)^2-v^2+8v}{16},&
 k&=\frac{r(3-v+u)}6,\\
 p&=\frac{(3-v+u)(5-v-u)}{48},&
 q&=\frac{(3-v+u)(L^2-u^2)}{864}.
 \end{aligned}                                                    \tag{H15}
$$
The determinant of $(v,u)\mapsto(r,k)$ is $k/8$. As $f=vrk/9$, substituting in the density gives a constant multiple of $dv\,du/\sqrt{v(L^2-u^2)}$. The entire region $0<v<1$, $-L<u<L$ is attained. Indeed $3-v>L$ and $5-v>L$ there, so $p,q$ and $f$ are positive. The cubic has three distinct real roots of sum one and positive second and third elementary symmetric functions. They must all be positive, since two negative roots and a positive sum would make the second symmetric function negative. Conversely on the regular compact image, $v>0$, $3-v+u>0$ since $k/r>0$, and $5-v-u>0$ since $p>0$. Thus $v<4$, and $L^2-u^2>0$ then forces $v<1$. This verifies the ranges needed for the change of variables. It follows that $v$ has the squared-uniform law and $u$ at fixed $v$ has the arcsine law on $[-L,L]$.

Write $a=5-v$, so $L^2=a^2-16$. By (H15),
$$
              x=X=\frac{L^2-u^2}{2(a-u)} .
$$
For $0<x<1-v$, the two inverse branches are $u_\pm=x\pm\sqrt{(a-x)^2-16}$. Put $D_\pm=a-u_\pm$, so $D_+D_-=16$. The sum of their density contributions is
$$
 \frac{\sqrt{D_+}+\sqrt{D_-}}{\pi\sqrt{2x}\sqrt{(a-x)^2-16}}
                =\frac1{\pi\sqrt{x(1-v-x)}}.
$$
The first equality for the density contributions follows from $L^2-u_\pm^2=2xD_\pm$ and $|du_\pm/dx|=D_\pm/\sqrt{(a-x)^2-16}$. This gives the conditional beta split of $1-v$ and proves the Dirichlet law.

Put $z=1-v-x$. At these two inverse points, $S=(a-u)/4$, as follows either from (H15) or from $r=(1-x)(a-u)/8$. Thus the two possible $S$ are reciprocal positive numbers satisfying $S+S^{-1}=2+z/2$. Their probabilities are proportional to their square roots. A useful polynomial conditional formula is
$$
       \mathbb E[r^n\mid X,V]
          =\left(\frac{1-X}{2}\right)^n C_n(1-X-V),\qquad n\ge0,
$$
where $C_0=1$, $C_1=1+z/2$, and $C_{n+2}=(2+z/2)C_{n+1}-C_n$. This follows by taking the probability-weighted sum of the two powers of $S$.

To obtain the uniform parameter, write $\rho=\sqrt S$ and $w=\rho-\rho^{-1}$. Then $z=2w^2$, and the probability of a choice of sign for a given $|w|$ is
$$
                          \frac{\rho}{\rho+\rho^{-1}}
                           =\frac{d\rho}{dw}.
$$
Since $\sqrt z$ is uniform on $[0,1]$, the signed $w$ has density $\sqrt 2\,d\rho/dw$ on $[-1/\sqrt 2,1/\sqrt 2]$. The variable $\rho$ is therefore uniform on $[1/\sqrt 2,\sqrt 2]$. The Dirichlet law also makes $B=X/(1-z)$ arcsine and independent of $z$. The branch probabilities for $S$ depend only on $z$, so the independence persists with $\rho$. The identities $1-z=(2-S)(2S-1)/S$ and $r=(1-X)S/2$ yield (H14). The orientation generator $J$ can be retained as well: conditional on $p,q$, inversion makes its sign equiprobable and $J^2=qv r k/9$.

**Corollary.** The two-height field $K(t,X,V)$ intersects $K[t,t^{-1},p,q]$ only in $K[t,t^{-1}]$ over any characteristic-zero field $K$.

Fix $X=x$. The other value of $p$ at fixed $x,v$ is $(2A(x)-x/3-p)/(1-3p)$, by the quadratic equation
$$
          v(1-3p)(1-x)=36\bigl(-p^2+2A(x)p-x/9\bigr)
$$
from (H3) and the definition of $V$. In terms of $r=1-3p$ the involution is $r\mapsto(1-x)^2/(4r)$. A regular polynomial becomes a polynomial in $r$ over $K(t,x)$ after $q=xp/9$. Such a polynomial is invariant under the reciprocal involution only if it is independent of $r$. Any rational function of $x=9q/p$ that is regular must also be independent of $x$: take the limit along every line through $(p,q)=(0,0)$, where the polynomial has one value for all $x$.

#### An endpoint algebra with the full angular function field

The uniform parameter in (H14) gives another joint endpoint identity. Fix $N\ge1$ and put
$$
   G(W)=(2-W)(2W-1),\qquad T=1+t,\qquad W=T^2S,\qquad
                         E=\frac{T}{t}G(W)^N .
$$
Use formal constant term at $t=0$, extending the trace to expressions that may also have a pole at $t=-1$. For $r\ge1$, $j\in\mathbb Z$ and $\ell\ge0$,
$$
 \begin{aligned}
 \Lambda(E^r W^j B^\ell)&=0,\\
 \Lambda(tE^r W^j B^\ell)
   &=\frac{(1/2)_\ell}{\ell!}\,
       \frac{\displaystyle\int_{1/\sqrt{2}}^{\sqrt{2}}
                         G(s^2)^{Nr}s^{2j}\,ds}{\sqrt 2-1/\sqrt 2}.
 \end{aligned}                                                     \tag{H16}
$$
Indeed, after substituting $s=T\sqrt S$, the integrand has prefactor $t^{-r}T^{r-1}$ and limits $T/\sqrt 2,T\sqrt 2$. Replacing the limits by the original endpoints changes the integral by a multiple of $t^{Nr+1}$, since $G(s^2)^{Nr}$ vanishes to order $Nr$ at both endpoints. The factor $s^{2j}$ is regular near this interval for every integral $j$. The prefactor then gives (H16). These identities make every element of the ideal generated by $E$ in $K[E,W,W^{-1},B]$ pure. By (H14) and $T=E/(E-G(W)^N)$, the fraction field of that algebra is the full angular Laurent function field.

**Theorem.** For this endpoint algebra,
$$
            K[E,W,W^{-1},B]\cap K[t,t^{-1},p,q]=K.
$$
The same conclusion holds for polynomials in $E$ whose coefficients are rational in $W,B$ and regular at the generic divisor $W=2$. More generally, if the coefficient of $E^r$ has a pole there of order at most $(N-1)r$, absorb that pole by using the generator with $N=1$ and multiplying its coefficient by $G(W)^{(N-1)r}$. Hence the intersection still consists of constants when this amount of pole is allowed in the joint endpoint coefficients.

Suppose $P$ belongs to both rings and write
$$
                      P=t^{-b}\sum_{i,j} a_{ij}(T)p^i q^j
$$
with $b\ge0$ and $a_{ij}\in K[T]$. In the endpoint coordinates, $S=W/T^2$ and $t=G(W)^N/(E-G(W)^N)$. A polynomial in $E$ with coefficients regular at generic $W=2$ is regular at the three divisors used below. They have $E=0$, $E=G(W)^N$, and $W=2$, with $B$ left as an independent variable.

At $E=0$, the variable $T$ has a simple zero at generic $W$. Formula (H14) gives leading terms proportional to $B S^2$ in $p$ and $B^2 S^3$ in $q$. A term with $a_{ij}$ of order $l$ at $T=0$ therefore has order $l-4i-6j$, with leading coefficient proportional to $W^{2i+3j}B^{i+2j}$. At a common order these monomials are distinct, since the two exponents determine $i,j$. Thus for every nonzero $a_{ij}$ one has $\operatorname{ord}_{T=0} a_{ij}\ge4i+6j$.

At $E=G(W)^N$, the variable $T$ has a simple pole at generic $W$, and $S$ tends to zero. Here $p\to(1-B)/3$ and $q$ has leading term $-2B(1-B)T^2/(27W)$. The order of a term in $T$ is $\deg a_{ij}-b+2j$. At a common order the leading coefficients have distinct powers $W^{-j}$ for distinct $j$, and for fixed $j$ the powers of $1-B$ from distinct $i$ are independent. It follows that $\deg a_{ij}\le b-2j$. The two bounds give
$$
                  \operatorname{ord}_{T=1}a_{ij}
                         \le b-4i-8j .
$$

At $W=2$, with $E,B$ generic, $t$ vanishes to order $N$ and $S-2$ vanishes to order one. For $N=1$ the leading coefficient of $S-2$ is a nonzero rational function of $E$, and for $N>1$ it is one. Hence $p$ and $q$ vanish to orders one and two, with leading coefficients proportional to $(1+3B)$ and $B(1+3B)$ respectively. If $e=\operatorname{ord}_{T=1}a_{ij}$, the order of a summand in $W-2$ is $Ne+i+2j-Nb$. At a common lowest order, group the summands by $D=i+2j$. Their leading coefficients are, up to nonzero factors over $K(E)$, the polynomials $(1+3B)^{i+j}B^j$ of degree $D$. Within a group of fixed $D$ they have distinct orders at $B=0$ and form an independent family with nonzero top-degree terms. To rule out cancellation across groups as well, observe that $Ne+D$ is fixed. Consequently different groups carry different powers of the leading coefficient of $t$ or of $S-2$. If $N>1$, the leading coefficient of $S-2$ is one and that of $t$ is a nonzero constant times $E^{-1}$. The groups then have distinct powers of $E$, and cannot cancel. For $N=1$, their coefficients in $E$ are proportional to $E^{-e}(1-\gamma/E)^D$ with a fixed nonzero $\gamma$. Since $e+D$ is fixed, these are proportional to a common power of $E^{-1}$ times $(E-\gamma)^D$, and are again independent. The same factors occur for every member of a fixed group because $e,D$ are fixed. Thus no lowest-order terms cancel.

If $i+j>0$, the degree bounds give
$$
        Ne+i+2j-Nb\ \le\ -(4N-1)i-(8N-2)j<0.
$$
Any such term would force a pole at $W=2$. Therefore $P$ is scalar. Its coefficient $a_{00}$ has degree at most $b$ and is divisible by $(T-1)^b$, again by regularity at $W=2$. Hence $P$ is constant. This proves the intersection theorem. The joint law (H14) also describes the regular ring outside this polynomial endpoint algebra.

#### Dilations depending on the independent coordinate

The conditional endpoint calculation allows the dilation to vary with $B$. For example, let $T_B=1+tA(B)$ with $A$ rational and bounded on the arcsine interval. Conditional integration in $\sqrt S$ treats $A(B)$ as constant. With a factor vanishing at both endpoints the same endpoint error gives pure rational phases. The following regularity obstruction applies even to these variable dilations.

**Proposition.** Let $F(p,q)$ be a nonconstant regular polynomial, expressed as $F(S,B)$ by (H14). If $A(B)\in K(B)$ is nonzero, $a\ge0$ and $b\in\mathbb Z$, the expression
$$
                  t^{-b}(1+tA(B))^a F((1+tA(B))^2S,B)
$$
is not in $K[t,t^{-1},p,q]$.

Write $F(S,B)$ as a Laurent polynomial in $S$. Its highest $S$ exponent is the weighted degree $d>0$ of $F(p,q)$ for weights $2,3$. Indeed, the leading terms of $p,q$ in (H14) are $-BS^2/3$ and $2B^2S^3/27$. Distinct pairs $(i,j)$ of monomial exponents at weight $d=2i+3j$ have distinct exponents $i+2j$ of $B$. Hence the coefficient $f_d(B)$ of $S^d$ is nonconstant and has degree at most $d$.

If the displayed expression is Laurent in $t$, its highest coefficient in $t$ is $A(B)^{2d+a} f_d(B) S^d$. This holds even if some lower $S$ powers give rational terms in $t$, by looking at $t=\infty$. For any finite pole of $A$, its fibre in $B$ has a generic affine point with $S$ a unit, by (H14). Regularity there would require $f_d$ to vanish to order at least $2d+a$, more than its degree. Thus $A$ is a polynomial. At the generic divisor $p^2=3q$ the variable $B$ has a pole and $S=2$ is a unit. The nonconstant polynomial $A^{2d+a} f_d$ gives a pole there, a contradiction. This leaves deformations involving both uniform and arcsine coordinates, or other ways to use their joint law, outside the endpoint exclusion.

### The icosahedral conditional differential

For the icosahedral formula (H6), put $y^2=-F(v,p)$ and let $C_v$ be the smooth projective curve of this function field over $\mathbb Q(v)$. The quartic is squarefree and has two rational points $I_+,I_-$ at infinity, distinguished by $y/p^2\to\pm4320$. The conditional differential
$$
                            \omega=\frac{4320p\,dp}{y}
$$
has simple poles at these two points with opposite residues of absolute value one.

**Proposition.** The divisor class $I_+-I_-$ has infinite order. In particular, after any algebraic extension of the height field and any finite cover of $C_v$, the pullback of $\omega$ cannot be a nonzero scalar multiple of a logarithmic differential $dz/z$ of an algebraic circle coordinate $z$.

Specialize to $v=1/2$, which gives a smooth elliptic curve. Taking $x=(p-1/40)^{-1}$ and $Y=x^2y$ gives the equation
$$
      Y^2=-\frac{6318}{5}x^3+253616x^2+4440960x+18662400 .
$$
The points at infinity of the quartic become $P=(0,4320)$ and $-P$, with the point at infinity of this cubic as identity. At the good primes $7,11$ the reductions have respective orders $13,9$. For explicit checks, modulo seven the cubic coefficients are $(2,6,6,1)$, $P=(0,1)$, and successive doubling gives $2P=(5,5)$, $4P=(3,6)$, $8P=(2,2)$. Adding $8P$ to $4P$ gives $-P$. Modulo eleven the coefficients are $(8,0,7,9)$, $P=(0,8)$, $2P=(8,5)$, $4P=(3,2)$, and $8P=(0,3)=-P$, while $3P=(10,4)\ne0$. In a good reduction the kernel has no prime-to-characteristic torsion. In an integral parameter at the identity the multiplication-by-$n$ series has leading term $n$ times the parameter, so it preserves a positive parameter valuation when $n$ is a unit. If $P$ were torsion, the order at seven would force a factor of thirteen upstairs. The order at eleven rules that out. Thus $P$ and $2P$ have infinite order. Torsion of $I_+-I_-$ on the generic curve would specialize to torsion at $v=1/2$, since the two points extend as sections of the smooth elliptic family there. This proves infinite order on $C_v$.

If a logarithmic coordinate existed on a finite cover, take the norm of the coordinate down to $C_v$. For a finite separable function field extension, tracing the logarithmic derivative gives the logarithmic derivative of the norm, by summing over the embeddings. Tracing the pulled-back $\omega$ multiplies it by the degree. Its poles and residues would then make the divisor of that norm a nonzero integral multiple of $I_+-I_-$. This is impossible. In particular the exact normalization by a circle differential used on the octahedral conditional conic has no algebraic counterpart for this vertex-height fibre. The coefficient-at-infinity joint trace (H6) still applies to arbitrary polynomial tests.

### Finite exclusion of affine Laurent phases on the two angular models

Write $(x,y)=(p_2,p_3)$ on the octahedral angular model and $(x,y)=(p,h)$ on the icosahedral angular model. Let $\lambda$ be the spherical trace with the laws above, and let $\Lambda$ be its combination with Laurent constant term.

**Theorem.** On either model let all coefficient functions below be affine in $x,y$. If the first ten positive moments of $A+B t+C/t$ vanish, then $A=0$ and $BC=0$. If the first nine positive moments of $t^{-1}+A+C t+D t^2$ vanish, then $A=C=D=0$. The same bounds hold after replacing $t$ by $t^d$ for any positive integer $d$. The resulting phases have one strict Laurent sign and are safe.

For the first family, the first moment makes the center $A=aX+bY$, with $X=x-\lambda(x)$ and $Y=y-\lambda(y)$. Write $E=BC=d_0+d_1X+d_2Y+d_3X^2+d_4XY+d_5Y^2$. The remaining moment equations are
$$
            \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}
              \binom{m}{2k}\binom{2k}{k}\lambda(A^{m-2k}E^k)=0
                   \qquad(2\le m\le10).                          \tag{H7}
$$
The product of two affine forms has a symmetric matrix of rank at most two after homogenizing with a constant variable. Its determinant, multiplied by four, is
$$
             4d_0d_3d_5-d_0d_4^2-d_1^2d_5+d_1d_2d_4-d_2^2d_3=0 .
$$

For the second family use the same center and separate affine functions $C,D$. In a contributing term with $k$ factors of $Ct$ and $\ell$ factors of $Dt^2$, the pole is used $k+2\ell$ times. The equations are
$$
          \sum_{2k+3\ell\le m}
           \frac{m!}{(m-2k-3\ell)!(k+2\ell)!k!\ell!}\,
                  \lambda(A^{m-2k-3\ell}C^k D^\ell)=0
                   \qquad(2\le m\le9).                           \tag{H8}
$$
A constant nonzero coefficient on the pole can be normalized to one by scalar Laurent rescaling.

For an exact finite calculation, reduce the two systems modulo $131$. The rational trace values are integral at this prime. For $B_3$, their denominators from the Dirichlet formula involve odd factors at most $61$ for the required total degree. For $H_3$, formula (H6) uses the factor $18662400$, a power of two from the square-root series, and odd factors at most $101$ in the final uniform-height moment. None is divisible by $131$. The [replay checker](verify_exceptional_affine_laurent_obstruction.py) constructs the coefficients over $\mathbb F_{131}$ and computes standard bases in degree-reverse-lexicographic order. In the displayed variable orders their leading ideals contain these pure powers on both models:
$$
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
\text{family}&\text{variables}&\text{exponents of leading pure powers}\\
\text{(H7)}&(a,b,d_0,d_1,d_2,d_3,d_4,d_5)&(2,4,6,6,6,6,7,13)\\
\text{(H8)}&(a,b,C_0,C_1,C_2,D_0,D_1,D_2)&(2,4,4,4,5,8,8,10)
\end{array}
$$
The first system is weighted homogeneous, with weights $1,1,2,\ldots,2$. The second has weights one on the center parameters, two on the coefficients of $C$ and three on those of $D$. Each modular zero set has dimension zero by the pure powers in the leading ideal. Since a nonzero weighted-homogeneous solution would give a one-dimensional scaling orbit, the geometric zero set in characteristic $131$ is the origin.

If either system had a nonzero characteristic-zero solution, the rational equations and the nonvanishing of one parameter would give a solution over an algebraic number field. At a place above $131$, scale the parameters by their weights so that they all become integral and at least one is a unit. This is possible after a finite ramified extension, by taking the scalar valuation to be minus the minimum of the parameter valuations divided by their weights. Weighted homogeneity preserves the equations. The mod-$131$ reduction would give a nonzero geometric solution, contradicting the leading-ideal computation. Thus the asserted vanishing holds in characteristic zero. In the trinomial case $BC=0$ forces one of $B,C$ to be identically zero since the angular ring is a domain. This calculation was replayed with Python 3.12.3 and Singular 4.3.2.

The affine coefficient condition concerns functions of the two basic angular invariants. It leaves higher polynomial degrees, other Laurent supports, and functions outside that angular subring open.

### Sharp growth of the conditional degree for real powers

The conditional laws (H3) and (H6) also quantify the degree cost of a polynomial regularization. For a nonzero real polynomial $G(p_2,p_3)$ let $d$ be its weighted degree for weights $(2,3)$. For a nonzero $G(p,h)$ use weights $(3,5)$. In either model the polynomial $\mathbb E[G^m\mid v]$ has degree at most $dm$, and has degree exactly $dm$ for every positive even $m$.

For $B_3$ write $p_2=v^2 X$ and $p_3=v^3 X/9$. The polynomial conditional moment formulas define an expansion at $v=\infty$. There the moments of $X$ have limits, with generating function $(1+z/12)^{-1/2}$ by substituting $z/v^2$ in (H3). They are the moments of $-Z/12$ for $Z$ of arcsine law on $[0,1]$. For $H_3$ write $p=v^3 X$, $h=36v^5X^2$. In the normalized inverse square root of (H6), substitute $1/p=z/v^3$. Only the coefficient linear in $z$ survives at infinity, giving $(1-z/135)^{-1/2}$. Its moments are those of $Z/135$. The substitutions in regular monomials bound the degree of each polynomial moment by its weight.

For a fixed weight, distinct monomials in the octahedral case give distinct powers $X^{i+j}$, since $2i+3j$ and $i+j$ determine the pair $(i,j)$. In the icosahedral case use $3i+5j$ and $i+2j$ instead. Thus the top weighted part becomes a nonzero real polynomial in $X$. The leading coefficient in degree $dm$ of the conditional moment is the expectation of its $m$-th power under the indicated limiting arcsine law. This is strictly positive for even $m$. In particular a choice of a real polynomial denominator-clearing multiplier cannot reduce this conditional degree by cancellation of its top weighted terms in the even moments.

### Prime obstruction for a single top angular monomial

Let $P$ be a rational-coefficient regular Laurent phase in the two basic angular invariants, with positive weighted angular degree $d$. Use weights $(2,3)$ in the octahedral coordinates $(p_2,p_3)$ and $(3,5)$ in the icosahedral coordinates $(p,h)$. Suppose its entire top weighted part has the form
$$
                      \gamma t^{-c}(1+t)^b x^i y^j,\qquad
                       \gamma\in\mathbb Q^\times,\qquad
                       1\le c\le b\le 2d,
$$
where $(x,y)$ is the chosen pair of coordinates and $b,c$ are positive integers. If $2dm+1$ is a sufficiently large prime $\mathfrak p$, then $\Lambda(P^m)$ has $\mathfrak p$-adic valuation exactly minus one. In particular no nonconstant polynomial in $P$ is pure.

Indeed, for each power of $t$ the conditional moment $\mathbb E[P^m\mid v]$ is polynomial in $v$ of degree at most $dm$. Its coefficients are integral at every sufficiently large prime, uniformly in $m$. This follows from (H3) and (H6): the inverse-square-root expansion uses binomial coefficients with only powers of two in their denominators, and chart substitution uses only the fixed denominators in the displayed coordinates. In either chart, $v^e$ has height moment $1/(2e+1)$. For $\mathfrak p=2dm+1$ the only possible pole at $\mathfrak p$ is therefore from the coefficient of $v^{dm}$.

Use the conditional leading law above to evaluate that coefficient. The top monomial becomes $9^{-j}X^{i+j}$ for $B_3$ and $36^j X^{i+2j}$ for $H_3$. Here $X=-Z/12$ or $Z/135$, respectively, for an arcsine variable $Z$ on $[0,1]$. Put $e=i+j$ or $e=i+2j$ accordingly. The normalized arcsine factor is $\mathbb E Z^{em}=4^{-em}\binom{2em}{em}$. Since $e\le d$, the binomial is a unit at $\mathfrak p$. The Laurent constant term on the top part is $\gamma^m\binom{bm}{cm}$, also a unit because $bm\le2dm<\mathfrak p$. Thus the pole at $\mathfrak p$ survives. More generally, if the scalar Laurent symbol on the single top monomial is $L(t)$, the same test is that $\operatorname{CT}_t L^m$ be a unit at $\mathfrak p$. In particular two nonzero scalar terms of opposite sign in the exponents give the obstruction on orders divisible by their minimal return length: the constant term is then a single binomial term.

For a nonconstant polynomial in $P$ of degree $r$, choose $\mathfrak p=2drm+1$ prime. The highest power $P^{rm}$ in the expansion of its $m$-th power is the only summand whose trace has a pole at $\mathfrak p$. If the polynomial has coefficients in a general characteristic-zero extension, one can first specialize its finitely generated coefficient ring to an algebraic number field while keeping its leading coefficient nonzero. The same proof applies at a place above $\mathfrak p$ after excluding finitely many primes of bad denominators or leading coefficient. Infinitely many primes of the required form follow, for example, by taking prime divisors of values of the cyclotomic polynomial $\Phi_{2dr}$. A prime divisor outside the factors of $2dr$ has the residue class one modulo $2dr$. Taking the input divisible by the primes dividing $2dr$ and by all previously listed primes excludes each of them, since the cyclotomic constant coefficient is one.

This applies in particular to the most direct regular denominator-clearings in both exceptional cases:
$$
 \frac{1+t}{t}\bigl(p_2-9(1+t)^2p_3\bigr)^N,\qquad
 \frac{1+t}{t}\bigl(h-36(1+t)^2p^2\bigr)^N
                         \qquad(N\ge1).
$$
Their top weights are $3N$ and $6N$, respectively. Neither taking a pure power nor modifying by a polynomial in the cleared seed can recover purity. The obstruction concerns the specified top Laurent symbol. A general regular counterexample can have a different, or multi-monomial, top weighted part.

The same prime calculation can use a supporting line of the angular weights at Laurent exponent zero. Write $P=\sum_j P_jt^j$ with rational or algebraic coefficients, and let $d_j$ be the angular weight of each nonzero $P_j$. Suppose $d_j+\sigma j\le D$ for a rational $\sigma$ and $D\ge1/2$. Assume equality occurs either only at $j=0$, or only at two indices $j_-<0<j_+$. At each equality index suppose the top part of $P_j$ is a single angular monomial. Then no nonzero power of $P$ is pure.

In the one-index case the sole top-weight contribution after Laurent constant term in $P^m$ is the $m$-th power of that monomial. In the two-index case take orders $m$ divisible by the primitive return length $(j_+-j_-)/\gcd(j_+,-j_-)$. The top contribution is the product of the two top angular monomials with the unique counts giving Laurent weight zero. Its coefficient is a binomial coefficient times powers of the two nonzero leading coefficients. In both cases its angular weight is $Dm$. All other contributing products have weight strictly less, since an index below the supporting line gives a strict drop and the Laurent exponents sum to zero. At large primes $\mathfrak p=2Dm+1$ the coefficient is a unit because $m<\mathfrak p$. This proves the simple-pole conclusion by the same arcsine leading-moment argument. One can require these orders to be multiples of any additional integer by using primes congruent to one modulo the resulting value of $2Dm$ per step.

For example, in a trinomial $B/t+A+Ct$ whose three coefficients each have a single top angular monomial, purity forces $2d_A=d_B+d_C$ whenever $A,B,C$ are nonzero. If the two sides are unequal, the top balance is attained by just the center or the opposite pair.

#### Three aligned top Laurent terms on either angular quotient

Use the basic angular coordinates and weights $(2,3)$ for $B_3$ and $(3,5)$ for $H_3$ as above. For a Laurent phase $P=\sum_j P_jt^j$, write $d_j$ for the weighted angular degree of each nonzero coefficient.

**Theorem.** Suppose a supporting line $d_j+\sigma j\le D$ attains equality just at $-e,0,e$ for an integer $e>0$. At those indices let the respective top coefficients be $aM_-$, $bM_0$, $cM_+$ with nonzero scalars and angular monomials satisfying $M_-M_+=M_0^2$. If $D\in\{2,3,4,5,6\}$, then every nonconstant polynomial in $P$ has infinitely many nonzero moments.

At Laurent weight zero, the part of maximum angular weight in $P^m$ is
$$
              M_0^m \operatorname{CT}_t(a/t+b+ct)^m .
$$
Only the supporting line can contribute at weight $Dm$, and a balanced product uses the same number of factors from the two opposite indices, so the monomial identity gives the formula. The conditional leading law is the same as in the prime obstruction above. At a good prime $p=2Dm+1$ the contribution of $M_0^m$ to the leading conditional coefficient is a unit, and only angular weight $Dm$ can cause a pole. It remains to find infinitely many primes of this form where the scalar constant term is a unit.

Put $h=2D$. After homogenizing the scalar Laurent expression, one has
$$
 \operatorname{CT}_t(a/t+b+ct)^m
       =[u^{p-1}v^{p-1}]\bigl(u^{h-1}v^{h-1}
                    (av^2+buv+cu^2)\bigr)^{(p-1)/h}.
$$
The binary form has degree $2h$. If the quadratic factor has a double root, the constant term is a nonzero scalar times $\binom{2m}{m}$, a unit at every large good $p$. Otherwise the degree-$h$ cyclic cover has four distinct branch points of multiplicities $(h-1,h-1,1,1)$. Its character-one summand survives normalization because every branch multiplicity is less than $h$. By the conic coefficient calculation above, a good ordinary prime with $p\equiv1\bmod h$ makes the displayed coefficient nonzero.

Here ordinary reduction follows from a decomposition of the Jacobian. Denote the deck generator by $g$. The permutation interchanging the two pairs of branch points by two transpositions is induced by a projective involution of the base over the splitting field. It lifts to an automorphism $\tau$ conjugating $g$ to $g^{-1}$, since it changes each branch exponent to its negative modulo $h$. Its square is a deck transformation. At an unramified fixed point on the base, the induced map of the fibre is $i\mapsto k-i$ in deck coordinates for some $k$, so its square fixes the fibre. Thus $\tau^2=1$ even for even $h$.

For each divisor $d>1$ of $h$, split the Jacobian up to isogeny by the rational idempotents in the algebra of the deck group. The primitive order-$d$ factor has dimension $\varphi(d)$. In fact every nontrivial deck character has dimension two in first cohomology: all four branch points remain totally ramified in each nontrivial cyclic quotient, and the curve of degree $d$ has genus $d-1$ by Riemann–Hurwitz. Subtraction for proper divisors gives dimension $2\varphi(d)$ in first cohomology at exact order $d$, with dimension two for each conjugate character. For $d=2$ the factor is elliptic. For $d>2$ the two involutions $\tau,g\tau$ exchange every pair of inverse characters and have invariant subspaces of half the dimension. Their intersection is fixed by $g$ and is zero on this factor. The images of the two averaging idempotents therefore give an isogeny from two abelian varieties of dimension $\varphi(d)/2$.

Here $h\in\{4,6,8,10,12\}$. Each such factor has dimension at most two and has ordinary reduction at a density-one set of primes after finite extension, by the elliptic case and Sawin's theorem used above. We can adjoin roots of unity and take degree-one ordinary primes in that extension. This proves the simple-pole conclusion for infinitely many $m$. For a polynomial of degree $r>0$ in $P$, take the primes congruent to one modulo $2Dr$ in the same way. The highest power $P^{rm}$ supplies the pole, while each smaller power has angular weight below the threshold. Coefficients in characteristic zero can be specialized to a number field preserving the supporting line, the nonzero coefficients and the branch configuration.

The same argument applies to an aligned face with more terms. Suppose the monomial exponent vector at an equality index $j$ is an affine function of $j$ with nonnegative integral intercept, and the Laurent polynomial of leading scalars on that face is
$$
                 \gamma t^{-r}(t-\alpha)^r(t-\beta)^r ,
\qquad \alpha\beta(\alpha-\beta)\gamma\ne0,\quad 0<r<2D .
$$
Assume $D$ is a positive integer and $2D/\gcd(2D,r)\in\{2,3,4,5,6,8,10,12\}$. In a balanced product the affine exponents again give a fixed monomial to power $m$. The binary form is now a scalar times $(uv)^{2D-r}((u-\alpha v)(u-\beta v))^r$. Extracting the common power $\gcd(2D,r)$ gives an effective cyclic degree $h'=2D/\gcd(2D,r)$ with four complementary branch multiplicities, each prime to $h'$. Its Jacobian splits by exactly the argument above, since every divisor of $h'$ has totient at most four. Hence the same prime pole excludes eventual vanishing for every nonconstant polynomial in $P$.

#### Aligned angular faces with unequal root multiplicities

**Corollary.** On either exceptional angular quotient, take a supporting line of positive integer central weight $D$ as in the preceding test. Suppose the monomial exponent vector at the equality indices is affine in the Laurent exponent with a nonnegative integral intercept. Let the leading scalar Laurent polynomial on that line be
$$
                 L(t)=\gamma t^{-a}(t-\alpha)^b(t-\beta)^c,
                 \qquad \alpha\beta(\alpha-\beta)\gamma\ne0,
$$
with positive integers $a,b,c$. Put $h=2D$ and suppose the entries of
$$
                  (h-a,\ h-b-c+a,\ b,\ c)
$$
lie strictly between zero and $h$. Divide the entries and $h$ by their common gcd, obtaining $b_1,\ldots,b_4$ and effective order $e$. If for every unit $i$ modulo $e$ other than $1,-1$ one has
$$
             \sum_{j=1}^4 (i b_j\bmod e) \ \in\ \{e,3e\},
$$
then every nonconstant polynomial in the phase has infinitely many nonzero moments. The residues in this test are in $\{1,\ldots,e-1\}$.

The affine condition again gives a common angular monomial to power $m$ on every balanced product on the face. At $p=2Dm+1$ the needed unit is the scalar constant term $\operatorname{CT}_t L(t)^m$. Homogenizing gives the listed branch exponents at $0,\infty,\alpha,\beta$. Removing the common power gives the cyclic cover of effective order $e$. Its character-one summand of $H^1(\mathcal O)$ is one-dimensional and its Frobenius coefficient gives the scalar constant term up to a unit. The primitive factor has relative rank two over $\mathbb Q(\zeta_e)$, since every primitive character ramifies at all four points. Its Lie multiplicities are computed by $-1+\sum_j\{i b_j/e\}$, up to conjugating the embeddings. The pair $i=1,-1$ has multiplicities $(1,1)$ and every other pair has multiplicities $(0,2)$ or $(2,0)$. The ordinary reduction lemma in the involution tests applies to this factor. For $e=2$ the factor is elliptic. After adjoining the required roots of unity, degree-one good ordinary primes supply the moment poles, including along any prescribed divisible sequence of orders as needed for polynomials in the phase.

Here are examples up to permutation, listed for the effective order.

| Effective order | Branch exponents |
|---|---|
| $5$ | $(1,3,3,3)$, $(2,2,2,4)$ |
| $9$ | $(1,5,5,7)$, $(2,4,4,8)$, $(3,5,5,5)$, $(4,4,4,6)$ |
| $30$ | $(2,13,18,27)$ |

For example, angular weight fifteen is available on either quotient. With a top monomial of that weight, the scalar symbol $t^{-28}(t-1)^{18}(t-2)^{27}$ gives the order-thirty case.

### The spherical height law for regular invariants

Let $\Gamma$ be a finite subgroup of $\mathrm O(3)$ acting on the round sphere $S^2$, and write $\mathbb E$ for normalized area. A real polynomial invariant $U$ on the sphere can have the law of the square of a uniform variable on $[0,1]$ exactly when $\Gamma$ preserves an unoriented line in $\mathbb R^3$. More generally, let $A,B$ be real regular invariants and $k\ge1$. If
$$
 \mathbb E[A^{m-j}B^j]=\frac{\mathbb E[A^m]}{2j+1}
       \qquad(m\in k\mathbb Z_{>0},\ 0\le j\le m),                 \tag{H7}
$$
then either $A=B=0$, or $A$ is a nonzero constant, $B/A$ has this law, and $\Gamma$ preserves a line. Thus the exact angular factorization used in the quaternion construction cannot hold with regular invariants on either exceptional angular sphere quotient.

For the height assertion, a preliminary local fact is that bounded density of a height in a neighborhood of a critical value forces every critical point over that value to be Morse and to be an extremum. In smooth coordinates at a degenerate critical point the Hessian is diagonal with at most one nonzero entry. The height difference is bounded by a constant times $x^2+(|x|+|y|)^3$. A rectangle of widths proportional to $\epsilon^{1/2}$ and $\epsilon^{1/3}$ maps to a strip of width $O(\epsilon)$ and has area comparable to $\epsilon^{5/6}$. For a Morse saddle the normal form $xy$ gives area at least a constant times $\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)$ in the same strip. Either bound contradicts bounded density. In particular a smooth height of globally bounded density on $S^2$ has exactly one minimum and one maximum. The Morse count gives their total as two, since there are no saddles. An invariant height of globally bounded density therefore forces a fixed point on $S^2$. [Milnor, Morse Theory, §§2–3](https://luis.impa.br/aulas/topdif/Milnor_MorseTheory.pdf)

Now suppose $U$ has the required squared-uniform law. It lies in $[0,1]$ by continuity and full support of area. Its density is bounded near all positive values, and the area of $\{U<\epsilon\}$ is $\sqrt{\epsilon}$. At a zero of rank two for the Hessian the zero is isolated. At rank one, solve $\partial_x U=0$ as a local real-analytic graph by the implicit function theorem. Every nearby zero lies on that graph. The restriction of $U$ to the graph is analytic in one variable, so an accumulation of zeros makes it identically zero there. The zero set is then a smooth curve near the point.

It remains to exclude a nonisolated zero $p$ of Hessian rank zero. In local coordinates centered at $p$, nonnegativity makes the cubic Taylor term zero as well, so the Hessian is $O(|x|^2)$. Curve selection gives an embedded analytic arc of zeros approaching $p$. Along any compact subarc a normal strip at a point $x$ of width $c\sqrt{\epsilon}/|x|$ lies in $\{U<\epsilon\}$ for all sufficiently small $\epsilon$. Indeed $U$ and its gradient vanish on the arc and the Hessian estimate applies along each sufficiently short normal segment. The area of these strips divided by $\sqrt{\epsilon}$ has lower bound a fixed positive constant times $\int ds/|x|$ on the subarc, using arclength $s$ in the chart. This integral is unbounded as the subarc approaches $p$, by comparison with the variation of $\log|x|$. This contradicts the exact area $\sqrt{\epsilon}$. The zero set is consequently a finite disjoint union of smooth circles and isolated points. [Milnor, Singular Points of Complex Hypersurfaces, Lemma 3.1](https://djvu.online/file/kCFrJq27uCPRi)

For every positive regular level, the superlevel components are disks. As the level decreases, a positive maximum starts a disk and there are no saddles to join components or add a hole. A positive minimum could only cap the boundary of a disk and make a closed component of the sphere, which is impossible since the zero set is nonempty. This argument uses only the finitely many critical points above the positive regular level. It shows that each component of $\{U>0\}$ contains one maximum and every loop in it contracts, by putting the loop in a positive regular superlevel. If there were at least two components of the zero set, one could draw a loop in its complement separating two zero points. Use a small circle about an isolated zero or a parallel copy of a zero circle on the side of the other component. This loop cannot contract in the sphere minus the two chosen points. Thus the zero set is one point or one circle. A single zero point is fixed by $\Gamma$. A single zero circle gives two complementary disks, so $\Gamma$ permutes their two maxima. The line spanned by the difference of these unit vectors is invariant. Conversely if $\Gamma$ preserves $\mathbb R e$ for a unit vector $e$, then $U=(e\cdot x)^2$ has the required law. [Milnor, Morse Theory, Theorems 3.1–3.2 and Remark 3.3](https://luis.impa.br/aulas/topdif/Milnor_MorseTheory.pdf)

To prove the factorization statement, suppose first that $A$ is nonconstant. Let $\ell$ be a positive even multiple of $k$ and set $R=A^\ell$. For each $j\ge0$, take a positive multiple $m_0$ of $\ell$ with $m_0\ge j$. Apply (H7) at $m=m_0+\ell r$ for all integers $r\ge0$. Density of polynomials on the compact range of $R$ gives
$$
 \mathbb E\left[\psi(R)\left(A^{m_0-j}B^j-\frac{A^{m_0}}{2j+1}\right)\right]=0
$$
for every continuous $\psi$. Take a nonzero regular value $c$ of $R$ in its image. Over a small interval of regular values around $c$, area disintegrates into smooth positive length densities on the fibres by the submersion formula. The integrals on these fibres are continuous in the value. Testing with $\psi$ supported in the interval gives the equality on the fibre $R=c$. There $A^{m_0}=c^{m_0/\ell}$ is a positive constant. Thus under normalized fibre measure the smooth function $B/A$ has the squared-uniform law, by uniqueness of moments for compactly supported measures. Its maximum is one, and at such a point the measure of $\{B/A>1-\epsilon\}$ on the one-dimensional fibre is at least a positive constant times $\sqrt{\epsilon}$ by Taylor's formula. The prescribed law gives $1-\sqrt{1-\epsilon}=O(\epsilon)$, a contradiction. If $A=0$ identically, taking $m=j$ even in (H7) gives $B=0$. A nonzero constant $A$ gives the required moments of $B/A$ directly, so the height result applies.

### Three Laurent terms with product determined by the center

Let $\Gamma\le\mathrm O(3)$ be finite with no fixed point on $S^2$, and let $A,B,C$ be real regular angular invariants. For
$$
                         P=A+B/t+Ct
$$
suppose $BC$ is a rational function of $A$. Then $P$ is pure only when $A=0$ and $BC=0$. The angular ring is a domain, so such a phase is one-sided in $t$ and safe. The same result holds over $\mathbb R$ for any $C^2$ function of $A$ defined on a neighborhood of its range.

If $A$ is constant, the first two moments give the conclusion directly. Otherwise its range is an interval $[a,b]$, and write $-4BC=W(A)$. A rational $W$ has no genuine pole on $[a,b]$ because $A$ is surjective onto this interval and $BC$ is bounded. Suppose that $P$ is pure. Its first moment gives $a<0<b$.

From a uniform sphere point and an independent uniform angle $\theta$, form a probability measure $\nu$ on $\mathbb R^3$ by the following rule. Where $W(A)\ge0$, take
$$
                y=(A,\sqrt{W(A)}\cos\theta,\sqrt{W(A)}\sin\theta).
$$
Where $W(A)<0$, take $y=(A+\sqrt{-W(A)}\cos\theta,0,0)$. In either case the constant term of $P^m$ is the angle average of $(y_0+i y_1)^m$, by the binomial expansion using the product of the opposite Laurent coefficients. This polynomial is harmonic. Its rotational average about the $y_0$-axis spans the rotationally invariant homogeneous harmonic polynomials of degree $m$. Indeed in a polynomial $\sum_j c_j y_0^{m-2j}(y_1^2+y_2^2)^j$ the Laplace equation determines all coefficients from $c_0$, and the displayed rotational average has $c_0=1$. Purity and rotational invariance therefore make $\nu$ integrate every harmonic polynomial to its value at zero.

Write $U_\nu(q)=\int|q-y|^{-1}d\nu(y)$. The Taylor expansion for large $q$ has homogeneous terms harmonic in $y$, so $U_\nu(q)=|q|^{-1}$. Consider the closed bounded set made of the radial disks at heights $z\in[a,b]$ with squared radius $\max(W(z),0)$, together with an axial segment containing all the axial samples. Its exterior is connected since any exterior point can go radially outward at fixed height and then around the set. Both potentials are harmonic on this exterior and therefore agree there.

If $W(z_0)<0$ at some interior $z_0\ne0$, then the axial part of $\nu$ has a density bounded below on an interval about $z_0$. Indeed a neighborhood of $A=z_0$ has positive probability, and for samples in a sufficiently small such neighborhood the arcsine laws of $A+\sqrt{-W(A)}\cos\theta$ all have uniformly positive density on a common interval about $z_0$. The potential of that interval diverges at least logarithmically at $q=(z_0,\delta,0)$ as $\delta$ decreases to zero. These points are exterior, where $|q|^{-1}$ stays bounded. Hence $W\ge0$ on $[a,b]$. If $W(0)=0$, then $\sqrt{W(z)}=O(|z|)$ near zero by nonnegativity and the $C^2$ bound. At $q=(0,\delta,0)$ the ratio $\delta/|q-y|$ is then uniformly bounded on the support and tends to zero off the origin. Dominated convergence and the exterior identity would give $\nu(\{0\})=1$. This is incompatible with the full nonconstant range of $A$. Thus $W(0)>0$ and zero is in the interior of the radial body.

We now show that the law of $A$ assigns $O(\epsilon)$ mass to a strip of width $2\epsilon$ about every height $c$ in its range. If $W(c)=W'(c)=0$, then $c\ne0$ and $\sqrt{W(z)}=O(|z-c|)$ near $c$. Take the exterior point $q=(c,\epsilon,0)$. Every sample in the strip has distance $O(\epsilon)$ from $q$. Its mass is at most $O(\epsilon)U_\nu(q)=O(\epsilon)$.

At every other height the surface $F(y)=y_1^2+y_2^2-W(y_0)=0$ is smooth in a band about that height. For $p$ on the support in the band let $n=\nabla F(p)/|\nabla F(p)|$ and $q=p+\delta n$. For sufficiently small $\delta>0$, $q$ is exterior. Taylor expansion on $F(y)=F(p)=0$ gives $(p-y)\cdot n\ge -C|p-y|^2$ uniformly for all support points $y$ and all $p$ in a smaller band. Consequently $|q-y|^2\ge \tfrac12|p-y|^2+\delta^2$. The normal derivative then gives
$$
  -\partial_n U_\nu(q)+2C U_\nu(q)
        \ \ge\ \int\frac{\delta}{|q-y|^3}\,d\nu(y)
        \ \ge\ \frac{\nu(\{|y-p|\le\delta\})}{8\delta^2}.
$$
The left side is uniformly bounded by the exterior identity and the positive distance from zero to the boundary. The ball bound dominates $\nu$ on the band by a constant times surface area, by covering with balls in the definition of surface measure. That area projects to height with bounded density $2\pi\sqrt{W+(W')^2/4}$. Endpoint circles or points have area zero. This proves the strip bound at the remaining heights.

Apply the critical-point argument in the preceding section to $A$. The strip bound precludes every degenerate critical point and every saddle. Hence $A$ has a unique minimum and maximum, which are fixed by $\Gamma$. This contradicts the hypothesis. In particular the obstruction includes constant $A^2-4BC$ and every polynomial relation $BC=f(A)$ on the octahedral and icosahedral angular models. For rational dependence the result transfers to real closed fields by embedding a finitely generated field of definition in $\mathbb R$. Nonconstancy, the identities, and the standard algebraic polyhedral model are preserved. The same transfer applies to (H7) because the angular trace commutes with base change. No sign or convexity hypothesis on the given coefficients is needed.


## Denominators in the two conditional coordinates

The octahedral Dirichlet chart and the icosahedral conditional differential give different obstructions to clearing denominators. The octahedral chart permits a change of the uniform coordinate. If the fixed-interval scalar moments vanish on every conditional fibre, the change can only produce a safe regular phase. The icosahedral conditional density does not admit transport between independent heights by finite algebraic correspondences, even modulo differentials with zero residues and logarithmic differentials.

### Conditional scalar purity on the full octahedral angular ring

**Lemma (Local rational moments).**

Let \(k\) be of characteristic zero and let \(R(y)\in k(y)\). Expand at \(y=\infty\). If the constant coefficients of \(R(y)^m\) vanish for all sufficiently large positive \(m\), then either \(R\) is regular and zero at infinity, or \(R\in y\,k[y]\). Each alternative gives zero constant coefficients at all positive orders.

For the proof, if \(R\) is regular at infinity the moments are its values there to the \(m\)-th power. Otherwise let \(d>0\) be its pole order there and set \(x=R(y)\), with local parameter \(\theta=x^{-1/d}\) after extending scalars to an algebraic closure. For \(z=1/y\), write \(z=a\theta\exp(g(\theta))\) with \(a\ne0\) and \(g\in\theta\overline k[[\theta]]\). Laurent constant term in \(y\) is the residue against \(dz/z\) at \(z=0\). In the parameter \(\theta\) this gives \(c_m=d m[\theta^{dm}]g\) for the \(m\)-th moment. The product of the \(d\) inverse branches \(y_j(x)\) going to infinity is therefore
\[
                 \prod_{j=1}^d y_j(x)
                  =b\,x\exp\!\left(-\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{c_m}{m x^m}\right)
                  \qquad(b\ne0).
\]
For eventual vanishing the sum in the exponential is a polynomial in \(1/x\) with zero constant. The product is algebraic over \(\overline k(x)\). Its logarithmic derivative can have only simple poles on the smooth curve of its function field. A nonconstant polynomial in \(1/x\) has differential with pole order at least two at every point over \(x=0\). Thus all \(c_m\) vanish and the product is \(b x\).

In the normal closure of \(R(y)-x=0\), fix a valuation over \(x=\infty\) with value \(-1\) on \(x\). There are precisely \(d\) roots of valuation \(-1/d\), coming from \(y=\infty\), and every other root has nonnegative valuation. Each conjugate product of these \(d\) roots still equals \(b x\). Its valuation is \(-1\) only for the same subset of roots. Transitivity on the roots of the rational map forces this subset to be all the roots. Hence infinity is the only pole of \(R\), and for a polynomial its constant moments are \(R(0)^m\). This proves the lemma, including descent to \(k\). For context, the inverse-branch expression is the local logarithmic case of the contour formulas studied in [Pakovich, Sections 2 and 6](https://arxiv.org/abs/0910.2105). The valuation argument above treats arbitrary finite poles of \(R\) in this local setting.

**Theorem (Octahedral conditional scalar purity).**

Use the octahedral chart (H14), and allow the full rotation-invariant angular ring \(K[p,q,J]\) from the conditional conic section above. Let \(P\in K[p,q,J][t,t^{-1}]\). Change variables by \(W=T^2 S\) with \(T=1+t\), and keep the independent arcsine coordinate \(B\) and its signed square root fixed. Write \(y=T/t\). If the fixed-interval conditional scalar constant coefficients at \(y=\infty\) of \(P^m\) vanish identically in the remaining coordinates for all sufficiently large \(m\), then the original Laurent support lies entirely in \(t^{>0}\) or entirely in \(t^{\le0}\). In the second branch its coefficient at \(t^0\) is reflection invariant. Over a formally real field every pure regular phase with this conditional property is safe, including against regular markers outside the angular ring.

Here the fixed-interval operator is the scalar residue after the substitution in the uniform integral, before the integration in \(W,B\) and before including the moving endpoint intervals. It is ordinary constant term at \(y=\infty\), since the uniform measure contributes \(1/T\) and
\[
                         \frac{dt}{tT}=-\frac{dy}{y}.
\]
The statement is an exclusion for pointwise conditional cancellation. Vanishing of the integrated pure moments alone does not imply that cancellation.

To prove the theorem, in (H14) put \(\rho=\sqrt S\) and take \(H\) with \(H^2=B(1-B)/5832\). The orientation coordinate satisfies
\[
 J=\frac{H}{\rho^3}(S-2)^2(2S-1)(2BS-B+1)(2BS^2-5BS+2B+S).
\]
Squaring this identity verifies it by substituting (H14) into
\(J^2=q(p^2-4p^3-4q-27q^2+18pq)\). The sign of \(H\) represents the orientation. With \(w=\sqrt W\), substitute \(\rho=w/T\), with \(T=y/(y-1)\) and \(t=1/(y-1)\). This embeds the angular Laurent function field into \(K(w,B,H)(y)\). Specializing \(T=1\) in the angular coordinates still gives an injection of the angular function field. The lemma shows that the transformed phase either vanishes at \(y=\infty\) or is a polynomial in \(y\). In the first branch the Laurent expansion in \(t\) starts at strictly positive exponent, by the injection at \(T=1\).

In the polynomial branch the transformed phase has no pole at \(y=1\), where \(T\) has a simple pole. Write the original phase
\[
                 P=t^{-b}\sum_{i,j}p^i q^j(a_{ij}(T)+J b_{ij}(T)),
                       \qquad a_{ij},b_{ij}\in K[T],
\]
using one common \(b\ge0\). At \(y=1\), with \(w,B,H\) generic, \(p\) tends to \((1-B)/3\), \(q\) has leading term \(-2B(1-B)T^2/(27w^2)\), and \(J\) has a pole of order three with nonzero leading coefficient. The even and odd parts in \(H\) cannot cancel a pole of one another. Within the even part a highest-order combination has distinct powers \(w^{-2j}\) for distinct \(j\). For each fixed \(j\), its coefficients after removing the common \(B^j(1-B)^j\) are distinct powers of \(1-B\) and are linearly independent. Divide the odd part by \(J\) to apply the same fact to its remaining factor. Absence of poles therefore gives
\[
                  \deg a_{ij}\le b-2j,\qquad
                  \deg b_{ij}\le b-2j-3 .
\]
Thus all original scalar exponents are nonpositive and the center comes only from the even part. If \(P\) is pure, its second moment is the normalized compact trace of the square of that center. The trace is strictly positive on nonzero squares over \(\mathbb R\). Over a formally real field the same conclusion holds in each finite set of angular monomials by positivity of the rational Gram matrices, or by specialization to a real model preserving a nonzero coefficient. So only negative exponents remain. In both branches, the strict Laurent sign forces eventual vanishing against every fixed regular multiplier, since each has bounded Laurent exponents.

This argument allows arbitrary rational dependence in the transformed scalar variable before imposing conditional vanishing. It uses the behavior at \(y=1\), without any assumption on the orders of poles in the angular parameters at the endpoints.

### No finite-correspondence transport for the icosahedral differential

**Theorem (Icosahedral finite-correspondence obstruction).**

Let \(v,w\) be algebraically independent over a characteristic-zero field and work over an algebraic closure of the joint parameter field. Use the smooth conditional quartics \(C_v,C_w\) from (H5) and their differentials \(\omega_v,\omega_w\) from the icosahedral conditional differential section. If \(f:D\to C_v\) and \(g:D\to C_w\) are finite morphisms from a smooth projective integral curve, then the pullbacks of \(\omega_v,\omega_w\) are linearly independent modulo the space spanned by differentials with zero residues and logarithmic differentials of rational functions on \(D\). In particular a generic change \(w=(1+t)^2v\) cannot transport these differentials by finite covers, even up to a nonzero scalar and those corrections.

For the proof, the quartics have nonconstant \(j\)-invariant. For a quartic \(a p^4+b p^3+c p^2+d p+e\), put
\[
 I=12ae-3bd+c^2,\qquad
 J_4=72ace+9bcd-27ad^2-27b^2e-2c^3.
\]
The invariant is \(j=6912 I^3/(4I^3-J_4^2)\), from the binary quartic Jacobian formula. Substituting the five coefficients of (H5) at \(v=1/2,1/3\) gives respectively \(32,27\) modulo \(101\), with denominators nonzero. [Fisher, Section 2](https://www.dpmms.cam.ac.uk/~taf1000/papers/minbqtc.pdf) Thus the two elliptic curves are not isogenous over the algebraic closure of the joint parameter field. Indeed a quotient of \(C_v\) by any finite subgroup is defined over an algebraic closure of the field of \(v\): its kernel lies in the torsion defined over that closure. The invariant \(j(w)\) is transcendental over the field of \(v\).

Take residues of a putative relation. If \(\Delta_v\) denotes the divisor of residues of \(\omega_v\), and similarly for \(w\), this gives a relation between \(f^*\Delta_v\), \(g^*\Delta_w\), and a linear combination of principal divisors of logarithmic terms. Pullback of residues includes the ramification index, exactly as for divisors. Pass to divisor classes tensored with the coefficient field over \(\mathbb Z\), and apply \(f_*\) to the resulting relation. It acts on the first term by \(\deg f\). On the second the homomorphism \(f_*g^*:\operatorname{Pic}^0(C_w)\to\operatorname{Pic}^0(C_v)\) is zero, since a nonzero homomorphism of elliptic curves is an isogeny. By the infinite-order computation for the two infinity points above, the class of \(\Delta_v\) survives tensoring in characteristic zero. The coefficient of \(f^*\omega_v\) must vanish. Applying \(g_*\) gives the other coefficient zero. The same argument allows extra corrections with zero residues by definition, including exact differentials. This leaves identities that couple the height and the conditional periods without transporting the conditional differential as a whole.

At a single generic height the only fibre automorphisms preserving the differential up to a scalar are the identity and the involution \(y\mapsto-y\). The residues force them to fix or exchange the two infinity points. An automorphism fixing both is an elliptic-curve automorphism fixing the origin and a point of infinite order, so it is the identity. Composing with the displayed involution handles the exchange.


## Compact homogeneous twists with a split commuting group

Let \(K\) be real closed and \(C\) a connected reductive group of compact type. For an affine geometrically homogeneous \(C\)-space \(X\), let \(A_X\) be its algebraic group of \(C\)-equivariant automorphisms.

**Theorem (Compact twists with positive commuting split rank).**

If \(A_X^\circ\) has positive \(K\)-split rank, the normalized trace on \(K[X]\) admits a regular Mathieu–Zhao counterexample. If the previously handled mechanisms of an active split rank-one action or an active compact geometric factor of rank at least two are absent, the construction below gives a fixed regular witness with nonzero marked moments at every positive order. In particular it applies when the split torus comes from a coupling between several compact rank-one factors.

Use the compact reference quotient \(C/L\) and the involution representative of its twist from the section on the commuting normalizer above. Thus \(L\) is defined over \(K\), \(N=N_C(L)\), and a representative \(n\in N(K)\) has \(n^2\in L\). Over \(K(i)\) the descent on the quotient is
\[
                    gL\longmapsto \sigma(g)n^{-1}L.
\]
The automorphism group \(A_X\) is the twist of \(A=N/L\) by conjugation by \(n\). The compact-type statements just used come from polar decomposition or [Adams–Taïbi, Theorem 1.1](https://otaibi.perso.math.cnrs.fr/galois.pdf), with the same finite algebraic transfer over real closed fields as in that section.

If the derived group of \(A_X^\circ\) has positive split rank, the commuting-action criterion already gives the obstruction. Otherwise \(A_X^\circ\) has a nontrivial split central torus. Its action is faithful and commutes with \(C\), so their product still acts geometrically transitively. If an active compact almost simple factor of \(C\) has geometric rank at least two, the long-root theorem applies to that product. Divide out ineffective normal factors and pass to a central isogeny. This preserves equivariant automorphisms, because the acting group maps onto the original effective group. For the rest of the proof we can therefore take
\[
                        C=C_0\times\prod_{i=1}^r C_i,
\]
where \(C_0\) is a compact torus and every \(C_i\) is the compact simply connected group of type \(A_1\). Apply the compact-reference construction to this presentation.

### The characters retained by the twist

Let \(L_i\) be the geometric projection of \(L\) in \(C_i\). It is reductive. Denote by \(I\) the indices with \(L_i\) noncentral and contained in a torus \(D_i\), by \(J_0\) the indices with \(L_i=N_{C_i}(D_i)\), and by \(I_0\) the indices with \(L_i\) central. The torus \(D_i\) in the first two cases is uniquely determined by \(L_i\): use the connected torus for a positive-dimensional projection or the centralizer for a noncentral finite cyclic one. In particular it is defined over \(K\). The other projections are either the whole \(C_i\) or finite noncyclic groups. The latter have finite normalizer, since the centralizer is finite and the normalizer modulo the centralizer acts faithfully on a finite group.

Put \(B_0=[L^\circ,L^\circ]\). Its projection to every full \(L_i=C_i\) is surjective, and its projection elsewhere is trivial. On the Lie algebra of \(B_0\), conjugation by the connected group \(N^\circ\) acts by inner automorphisms. Its centralizer has no Lie algebra projection on any full index, since it centralizes the full \(C_i\) there. The factors \(C_i\) for \(i\in I_0\) centralize \(L\) and belong to \(N^\circ\). Together with the finite-normalizer observation this gives
\[
                N^\circ=B_0\cdot\Big(\prod_{i\in I_0}C_i\Big)\cdot M,
       \qquad M=\left(N\cap\Big(C_0\times\prod_{i\in I\cup J_0}D_i\Big)\right)^\circ .
\]
To see the equality, use the Lie algebra sum of \(\operatorname{Lie}(B_0)\) and its centralizer in \(\operatorname{Lie}(N)\), then remove the direct factors for \(I_0\). The connected torus directions left are precisely \(\operatorname{Lie}(M)\). These connected subgroups commute and their product is a closed connected subgroup with the full Lie algebra. In particular \(M\) surjects onto the abelianization \(Z=A^\circ/[A^\circ,A^\circ]\).

The elements of \(L\) act on the rational cocharacters of \(M\) by coordinate signs. They act trivially on \(Z\). Average over this finite sign group. The resulting projection deletes the directions indexed by \(J_0\), because for each such index some element of \(L\) acts by its Weyl involution. The directions of \(C_0\) and of \(D_i\) for \(i\in I\) are fixed. These tori are already contained in \(M\), since they centralize \(L\). Consequently the map on rational cocharacters from \(M\) to \(Z\) factors through the projection to \(C_0\times\prod_{i\in I}D_i\).

The reference \(Z\) is a compact torus, with conjugation \(-1\) on its character lattice. Its twist has a split direction, so there is a nonzero character of \(Z\) on which \(\operatorname{Int}(n)\) acts by \(-1\). Pull it back to \(M\). By the factorization just proved, its pullback depends only on \(D_i\) for \(i\in I\) where \(n_i\) acts by the Weyl involution. Call these the flipped indices. Indeed \(n\) acts trivially on \(C_0\) and by a coordinate sign on each \(D_i\), so the anti-invariant character has no other support. Each flipped \(D_i\) is a direct factor of \(C_0\times\prod_{i\in I}D_i\), so its character coordinate \(\lambda_i\) is integral. The resulting character \(\lambda\) of the product of flipped tori is trivial on the projection of \(L^\circ\): the connected center of \(L^\circ\) lies in \(M\), and \(B_0\) has no projection on a flipped index. After a positive integral multiple it is trivial on the entire projection of \(L\), since the group of components is finite. At least one \(\lambda_i\) is nonzero.

Right translations by the product of flipped \(D_i\) commute with \(L\). On the twist, conjugation acts by the compact involution followed by the Weyl involution, so the corresponding right torus is split. Any kernel of this action is immaterial for the nonzero weight \(2\lambda\) constructed next.

### The regular all-order witness

On each flipped factor choose the usual compact \(\mathrm{SL}_2\) model over \(K(i)\) with \(D_i\) diagonal and compact conjugation \(g\mapsto(\bar g^{\mathsf T})^{-1}\). Write \(n_i=\operatorname{diag}(\eta_i,\eta_i^{-1})\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) with \(\eta_i\bar\eta_i=1\). Set
\[
 g_i^{-1}\operatorname{diag}(i,-i)g_i
      =\begin{pmatrix}a_i&u_i\\w_i&-a_i\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad A_i=\eta_i^{-1}u_i,\quad B_i=-\eta_i w_i,\quad v_i=-a_i^2 .
\]
Under \(g_i\mapsto\sigma(g_i)n_i^{-1}\) the matrix goes to its entrywise conjugate conjugated by \(\operatorname{diag}(\eta_i,\eta_i^{-1})\). Thus \(A_i,B_i,v_i\) are compatible with reality for this semilinear map. The identity from the determinant is \(A_i B_i=1-v_i\). For right translation by \(\operatorname{diag}(s,s^{-1})\), the weights of \(A_i,B_i\) are \(-2,2\) respectively.

With \(\lambda_i^+=\max(\lambda_i,0)\) and \(\lambda_i^-=\max(-\lambda_i,0)\), put
\[
 U=\prod_i B_i^{\lambda_i^+}A_i^{\lambda_i^-},
 \qquad V=\prod_i A_i^{\lambda_i^+}B_i^{\lambda_i^-}.
\]
Each product descends through \(L\) by the character condition, and through the twist by the reality just checked. The \(v_i\) descend as weight-zero functions. The products are nonzero regular functions of weights \(2\lambda,-2\lambda\). Choose an index \(j\) with \(k=|\lambda_j|\ge1\) and put
\[
 D=\prod_{i\ne j}(1-v_i)^{|\lambda_i|},\qquad
 P=V+D\left.
       \frac{(1+t)(1-(1+t)^2v_j)^k-(1-v_j)^k}{t}
             \right|_{t=U},\qquad Q=U .
\]
The displayed divided difference is polynomial. The relation \(UV=D(1-v_j)^k\) gives the expression
\[
                   P=D\,U^{-1}(1+U)(1-(1+U)^2v_j)^k
\]
in the localization. The normalized trace is invariant under the commuting torus by uniqueness. Weight zero selects the scalar Laurent constant term in \(U\), all the \(v_i\) having weight zero.

To calculate the coefficient traces, use the compact reference group over the complex numbers and pull back along its orbit map. On each factor, the diagonal coordinate of the conjugated \(\operatorname{diag}(i,-i)\) is \(i\) times a uniform variable on \([-1,1]\), by the ternary angular law. The \(v_i\) are squares of independent such variables by product Haar measure. The resulting moment formulas have rational coefficients and hold over \(K\) by base change from a finitely generated model. Integrate first in \(\sqrt{v_j}\) after writing \(t=U\). The factor in the \(m\)-th pure moment is
\[
 t^{-m}(1+t)^{m-1}
       \int_0^{1+t}(1-s^2)^{km}\,ds.
\]
Replacing the upper endpoint by one changes the integral by a multiple of \(t^{km+1}\). The factor \((1+t)^{m-1}\) has no term of order \(m\) and its coefficient at order \(m-1\) is one. The other factors contribute the independent moments of \(D^m\). Thus for every \(m\ge1\),
\[
 \Lambda_X(P^m)=0,\qquad
 \Lambda_X(QP^m)=
             \prod_{i:\lambda_i\ne0}
             \frac{4^{|\lambda_i|m}((|\lambda_i|m)!)^2}
                  {(2|\lambda_i|m+1)!}.
\]
This establishes the fixed witness over the ground field.

For example, take \(C=\mathrm{SU}_2^2\), the diagonal maximal torus \(L\) embedded in its two factors, and the twist represented by simultaneous Weyl elements in both factors. Their square lies in \(L\). The right torus quotient has split rank one after twisting and uses the character \(\lambda=(1,-1)\). The two individual homogeneous projections have torus stabilizers, with finite commuting automorphism groups. The split direction and the regular witness come from the coupling. In this example the marked moment is the square of \(4^m(m!)^2/(2m+1)!\).

The theorem leaves compact twists whose commuting identity component is compact and whose cocycle is nontrivial. The norm-minus-one quaternion torsor is an instance. Its central involution induces the identity automorphism on the commuting group, so a split direction does not arise there.


### Reduction of the mixed real-closed case without rational points

**Theorem (Mixed reduction without rational points).**

Let \(K\) be real closed, \(C\) connected reductive of compact type, and \(T\) a split torus. Let \(X\) be affine and geometrically homogeneous under \(T\times C\). Assume \(T\) and \([C,C]\) both act nontrivially. If \(C\) acts geometrically transitively, there is a regular witness. Otherwise \(X\) has a regular witness whenever both exceptional compact products \((\mathrm{SU}_2/2O)\times\mathbb G_m\) and \((\mathrm{SU}_2/2I)\times\mathbb G_m\) do. The second assertion reduces each such \(X\) either to the adjoint angular calculation, the unoriented axis calculation, or a single one of those two products. No \(K\)-point of \(X\) is needed.

Indeed, after choosing a geometric point with stabilizer \(H\), the two invariant quotients are
\[
        Y=X/\!/C,\qquad Z=X/\!/T,\qquad
        Y_{\overline K}=T_{\overline K}/\operatorname{pr}_T H,\quad
        Z_{\overline K}=C_{\overline K}/\operatorname{pr}_C H.
\]
The projected groups are reductive because they are images of the geometric reductive stabilizer. The quotients are affine homogeneous by Matsushima's criterion. These geometric quotient maps descend by invariant projection and base change. Their product \(X\to Y\times Z\) is geometrically surjective, since the two projection coset coordinates can be chosen independently. Its pullback preserves normalized traces. The action of \(D=[C,C]\) is nontrivial on \(Z\). For if \(D\subseteq\operatorname{pr}_C H\) geometrically, it is contained in the derived group of \((\operatorname{pr}_C H)^\circ\). The latter is the image of \([H^\circ,H^\circ]\), which lies in \(\{1\}\times C\). Then \(D\subset H\), and normality contradicts activity on \(X\).

When \(Y\) is a point, \(C\) acts geometrically transitively on \(X\) and the nontrivial image of \(T\) gives a split torus of \(C\)-equivariant automorphisms. The preceding theorem applies. Otherwise \(Y\) is a torsor of positive dimension under a quotient of the split torus \(T\), so it has a \(K\)-point by Hilbert 90. A nontrivial character, after choosing that point as origin, gives an equivariant map to \(\mathbb G_m\) preserving normalized traces. It remains to consider \(Z\times\mathbb G_m\).

Choose an active compact rank-one subgroup \(S\) of \(C\). Its general geometric stabilizers on \(Z\) lie in a single class of proper reductive subgroups by the principal-orbit argument. For a central stabilizer, restriction to a closed geometric orbit supplies an adjoint covariant, with no rational point needed. Use the angular identity with the external split variable. Over the complex numbers the compatible compact orbit provides a real compact covariant with nonzero radius, and specialization of the radial moment recurrence gives nonvanishing at infinitely many orders over \(K\).

For a noncentral stabilizer, attach the torus normalizer, \(2O\), or \(2I\), as in the no-point proof. Each attached group is conjugate geometrically to a \(K\)-defined compact model of its type. For the torus normalizer take one standard compact maximal torus. For the two finite groups take the standard binary rotation groups in the compact rank-one group. Their embeddings are defined over a real algebraic subfield and all embeddings of a given type are geometrically conjugate. The targets are self-normalizing, so the same Galois argument gives the rational map from \(Z\) to the corresponding \(S/M_0\). For the torus normalizer use the regular squared axis function on \(S/M_0\) and the angular identity with the external variable. For either exceptional target use its regular phase and marker if they exist. Pull back the finitely many coefficients in the split variable. A common invariant denominator on \(Z\) clears their poles. Since this denominator is independent of the split variable, the projector in \(S\) and the constant term in the split variable give the target moments times the denominator powers. The compatible compact orbit gives denominators with nonzero powers over the complex numbers. The recurrence and arithmetic specialization applied to the full marked product then preserve infinitely many nonzero ground-field moments. This completes the reduction.

For a general connected reductive acting group over a real closed field, the active split rank-one theorem handles every active noncompact semisimple factor. After dividing out inactive factors a central isogeny of the residual group has the form \(T\times C\) above. If the derived action is trivial the trace is Mathieu–Zhao by scalar extension and the Laurent theorem. If the split central action is trivial the case is a compact homogeneous form. Forms with a point have strict square positivity, and those with a noncompact commuting identity component have an obstruction by the preceding theorem. Thus to settle the connected real-closed classification it suffices to settle the two compact exceptional products and the compact homogeneous twists with no rational point and compact commuting identity component. Both reductions concern the trace on regular functions.


## Joint moments from changing the arrangement weights

Fix a finite set of real hyperplanes through the origin in \(\mathbb R^3\) with unit normals \(\alpha_i\), and put \(\ell_i(x)=\alpha_i\cdot x\). Take positive real weights \(\tau_i\) of sum one and a real direction \(\delta_i\) of sum zero. On the unit sphere, away from the hyperplanes, define
\[
 V=\sum_i\tau_i\frac{\alpha_i}{\ell_i},\qquad
 D=\sum_i\delta_i\frac{\alpha_i}{\ell_i},\qquad
 U=|V|^{-2},\qquad A=U V\cdot D,\qquad B=U|D|^2 .
\]
For an invariant arrangement and orbit-constant weights, \(U,A,B\) are invariant. A line of weights that gives positive weights to every hyperplane at the base point suffices, even if the separate endpoint arrangements have different hyperplanes.

**Theorem (Joint moments from the weight direction).**

For sufficiently small real \(z\), the following exact identities hold under normalized area, where \(m>0\) may be real:
\[
 \mathbb E\!\left[U^m(1+2zA+z^2B)^{-m}\right]=\frac{1}{2m+1},
 \qquad
 \mathbb E\log(1+2zA+z^2B)=0 .
\]
In particular, for each integer \(n\ge1\),
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
 \frac{(-1)^{n-j}(m)_{n-j}}{j!(n-2j)!}
                  \mathbb E\!\left[U^m(2A)^{n-2j}B^j\right]=0 .
\]
For almost every \(v\in(0,1)\), the first two conditional consequences are
\[
 \mathbb E[A\mid U=v]=0,\qquad
 \mathbb E[A^2\mid U=v]
       =\frac{\sqrt v}{2}\int_v^1
                   \frac{\mathbb E[B\mid U=u]}{u^{3/2}}\,du .
\]

For uniform bounds permitting the derivatives, choose one unit interior vector in each chamber of the hyperplane arrangement. In its chamber, all the signed normals \(\operatorname{sgn}(\ell_i)\alpha_i\) have a positive dot product with that vector. The minimum of these dot products over the finite chamber list is some \(c>0\). Thus \(|V|\ge c\sum_i\tau_i/|\ell_i|\). The triangle inequality bounds \(|D|/|V|\) by \(c^{-1}\max_i|\delta_i|/\tau_i\). This bounds \(A,B\) and gives a disk of convergence for the powers and logarithm with uniform bounds on every smaller disk.

The inverse squared norm of \(V+zD\) is \(U/(1+2zA+z^2B)\). For small real \(z\) its height law is (H1) by [Pillai–Meng, Theorem 2.2](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1505.01957), applied to the positive weights \(\tau_i+z\delta_i\). The law of \(U\) has density \(1/(2\sqrt u)\) on \((0,1)\), giving the first identity. The logarithms of the two heights are integrable with identical expectations. Subtract them for the second identity. The coefficient formula follows by expanding the binomial series inside the integral, using the bounds just proved.

The first coefficient identity and density of polynomials on \([0,1]\) give the conditional mean zero. Write \(\psi(v)=\mathbb E[A^2\mid U=v]\) and \(\phi(v)=\mathbb E[B\mid U=v]\).
